# RCE Anesthesiology Practice Exam — Set 01 — Rationale First Pass

> Editorial first pass. Evidence-bound explanations are preserved. Items marked for second pass have not been rewritten. Human specialist review remains pending.

# Paper 1

## S01-Q001

Domain 1 · Assessment and prediction

A parturient had Mallampati class II before labour and class III on reassessment before urgent anaesthesia. Which airway-planning interpretation is most appropriate?

A. Use Mallampati with other airway assessments, not as a sole predictor.

B. Reassess Mallampati during pregnancy and labour.

C. Treat a higher Mallampati score as increasing intubation difficulty.

D. Assess mandibular space with thyromental distance.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Use Mallampati with other airway assessments, not as a sole predictor.

Use the Mallampati score together with other airway assessments rather than as a sole predictor of difficult intubation.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize that bedside airway assessment findings are imperfect individually and should be integrated to anticipate difficult laryngoscopy.

**B:** Reassess Mallampati during pregnancy and labour.

Mallampati scores can change during pregnancy and during labor.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Reassess Mallampati during pregnancy and labour.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize that bedside airway assessment findings are imperfect individually and should be integrated to anticipate difficult laryngoscopy.

**C:** Treat a higher Mallampati score as increasing intubation difficulty.

Increasing Mallampati scores are associated with increasing difficulty with laryngoscopy and tracheal intubation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Treat a higher Mallampati score as increasing intubation difficulty.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize that bedside airway assessment findings are imperfect individually and should be integrated to anticipate difficult laryngoscopy.

**D:** Assess mandibular space with thyromental distance.

The thyromental distance estimates the size of the mandibular space available for tongue displacement.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Assess mandibular space with thyromental distance.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize that bedside airway assessment findings are imperfect individually and should be integrated to anticipate difficult laryngoscopy.

**Evidence sources:**

- Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf — PDF p. 764, passage 273626

## S01-Q002

Domain 1 · Assessment and prediction

Which airway examination finding most directly reflects both neck mobility and retrognathia?

A. Mallampati grade

B. A short thyromental distance

C. Limited mouth opening

D. A beard

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Mallampati grade

Mallampati grade describes mouth opening, tongue size, and pharyngeal space rather than directly reflecting neck mobility and retrognathia.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Mallampati grade” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply airway examination findings to identify a patient at increased risk of difficult laryngoscopy.

**B — correct:** A short thyromental distance

The thyromental distance reflects neck mobility and degree of retrognathia, making it the best match.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Apply airway examination findings to identify a patient at increased risk of difficult laryngoscopy.

**C:** Limited mouth opening

Limited mouth opening can make blade insertion and tongue displacement difficult, but it does not directly reflect both features.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Limited mouth opening” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply airway examination findings to identify a patient at increased risk of difficult laryngoscopy.

**D:** A beard

A beard primarily predicts difficulty obtaining a mask seal.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “A beard” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply airway examination findings to identify a patient at increased risk of difficult laryngoscopy.

**Evidence sources:**

- Barash 9e (2023).pdf — PDF p. 2302, passage 247157

## S01-Q003

Domain 1 · Preoxygenation and mask ventilation

In an obese patient, which positioning intervention was shown to increase time to desaturation compared with supine preoxygenation without positive airway pressure?

A. Use 10 cm H2O CPAP during supine preoxygenation.

B. Use 7.5 cm H2O CPAP during 3 minutes of supine preoxygenation.

C. Use a 25-degree head-up position for preoxygenation rather than supine without positive airway pressure.

D. Combine CPAP during preoxygenation with PEEP and ventilation after induction.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: SECOND PASS RESOLVED**

**Answer: C**

**A:** Use 10 cm H2O CPAP during supine preoxygenation.

Supine preoxygenation with 10 cm H2O CPAP produced higher post-intubation PaO2 and less atelectasis, but this is a positive-pressure intervention with different reported outcomes rather than the positioning comparison requested.

**B:** Use 7.5 cm H2O CPAP during 3 minutes of supine preoxygenation.

Supine preoxygenation with 7.5 cm H2O CPAP did not change time to desaturation to an SpO2 of 90% and is not a positioning intervention.

**C — correct:** Use a 25-degree head-up position for preoxygenation rather than supine without positive airway pressure.

A 25-degree head-up position is the best answer because it increased time to desaturation compared directly with supine preoxygenation without positive airway pressure.

**D:** Combine CPAP during preoxygenation with PEEP and ventilation after induction.

Combining CPAP during preoxygenation with PEEP and postinduction ventilation increased nonhypoxemic apnea duration, but it changes positive-pressure management rather than patient position.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 2136, passage 290554

## S01-Q004

Domain 1 · Preoxygenation and mask ventilation

When time permits before urgent induction in a term pregnant patient at high risk of rapid desaturation, which preoxygenation approach provides the most reliable apnea-desaturation protection?

A. Eight deep breaths over 1 minute

B. No formal preoxygenation because pregnancy already increases oxygen consumption

C. Four maximal breaths over 30 seconds

D. Three minutes of tidal-volume breathing through a tight-fitting face mask

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: D**

**A:** Eight deep breaths over 1 minute

Eight deep breaths over 1 minute appear to protect against apnea desaturation better than four deep breaths over 30 seconds.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Eight deep breaths over 1 minute” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recall the physiologic mechanism by which preoxygenation prolongs safe apnea during induction.

**B:** No formal preoxygenation because pregnancy already increases oxygen consumption

Pregnancy-associated reduction in functional residual capacity and increased oxygen consumption accelerate hypoxemia during apnea.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “No formal preoxygenation because pregnancy already increases oxygen consumption” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recall the physiologic mechanism by which preoxygenation prolongs safe apnea during induction.

**C:** Four maximal breaths over 30 seconds

Four maximal breaths over 30 seconds can achieve a similar PaO2 but do not provide the same protection from rapid desaturation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Four maximal breaths over 30 seconds” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recall the physiologic mechanism by which preoxygenation prolongs safe apnea during induction.

**D — correct:** Three minutes of tidal-volume breathing through a tight-fitting face mask

Three minutes of tidal-volume breathing through a tight-fitting mask is the ideal preoxygenation method described for this setting.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recall the physiologic mechanism by which preoxygenation prolongs safe apnea during induction.

**Evidence sources:**

- Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf — PDF p. 600, passage 272842

## S01-Q005

Domain 1 · Airway devices and intubation

According to the Difficult Airway Society (DAS) 2025 guidelines, which device is designated first line to facilitate tracheal intubation during induction in an adult?

A. Video laryngoscope

B. Direct laryngoscope

C. Optical stylet

D. Flexible bronchoscope

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Video laryngoscope

The Difficult Airway Society 2025 guidelines state that a videolaryngoscope should be used first line to facilitate tracheal intubation.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Select an airway device strategy that improves first-pass tracheal intubation when direct laryngoscopy is difficult.

**B:** Direct laryngoscope

The guideline names videolaryngoscopy, not direct laryngoscopy, as the technique to be used first line; the recommending sentence explicitly contrasts videolaryngoscopy 'compared with direct laryngoscopy.' Direct laryngoscopy is not the device the guideline designates first line.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Direct laryngoscope” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select an airway device strategy that improves first-pass tracheal intubation when direct laryngoscopy is difficult.

**C:** Optical stylet

The guideline describes the optical stylet as an additional device that intubation 'can also be achieved with,' listed after and distinct from the first-line videolaryngoscope recommendation, not as the designated first-line device.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Optical stylet” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select an airway device strategy that improves first-pass tracheal intubation when direct laryngoscopy is difficult.

**D:** Flexible bronchoscope

The guideline places the flexible bronchoscope in an adjunct role ('A stylet, bougie, or flexible bronchoscope should be used with a hyperangulated videolaryngoscope blade'), used together with a videolaryngoscope rather than named as the first-line device itself.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Flexible bronchoscope” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select an airway device strategy that improves first-pass tracheal intubation when direct laryngoscopy is difficult.

**Evidence sources:**

- Cote 7e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 607, passage 185312
- DAS_2025_Unanticipated_Difficult_Intubation_Adults.pdf — PDF p. 6, passage 317270
- DAS_2025_Unanticipated_Difficult_Intubation_Adults.pdf — PDF p. 7, passage 317271
- DAS_2025_Unanticipated_Difficult_Intubation_Adults.pdf — PDF p. 8, passage 317276

## S01-Q011

Domain 2 · Cardiovascular physiology and hemodynamics

Along a fixed cardiac function curve, which isolated change increases stroke volume?

A. Increase heart rate while remaining on the same cardiac function curve.

B. Increase preload while remaining on the same cardiac function curve.

C. Increase myocardial contractility and shift to a different curve.

D. Decrease afterload and shift to a different curve.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Increase heart rate while remaining on the same cardiac function curve.

Heart rate, preload, myocardial contractility, and afterload are the four factors determining cardiac output.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Increase heart rate while remaining on the same cardiac function curve.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate preload, afterload, and contractility changes to predict cardiac output in a perioperative hemodynamic state.

**B — correct:** Increase preload while remaining on the same cardiac function curve.

Increasing preload increases stroke volume on the cardiac function curve.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate preload, afterload, and contractility changes to predict cardiac output in a perioperative hemodynamic state.

**C:** Increase myocardial contractility and shift to a different curve.

The cardiac function curve relates preload to stroke volume or cardiac output.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Increase myocardial contractility and shift to a different curve.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate preload, afterload, and contractility changes to predict cardiac output in a perioperative hemodynamic state.

**D:** Decrease afterload and shift to a different curve.

The end-systolic pressure-volume relationship is represented as a straight line in the pressure-volume description.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Decrease afterload and shift to a different curve.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate preload, afterload, and contractility changes to predict cardiac output in a perioperative hemodynamic state.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 348, passage 282112

## S01-Q012

Domain 2 · Cardiovascular physiology and hemodynamics

A patient has an aortic diastolic pressure of 65 mm Hg and a left-ventricular end-diastolic pressure of 15 mm Hg. What is the left-ventricular coronary perfusion pressure?

A. 80 mm Hg

B. 15 mm Hg

C. 50 mm Hg

D. 65 mm Hg

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: C**

**A:** 80 mm Hg

Although lower afterload can reduce wall tension and myocardial oxygen demand, severe hypotension can jeopardize coronary perfusion pressure; in left ventricular hypertrophy with coronary disease, the supply deficit is the dominant concern.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “80 mm Hg” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recall the determinants of myocardial oxygen supply and demand relevant to anesthetic management.

**B:** 15 mm Hg

The stated heart rate is not tachycardic; moreover, tachycardia shortens diastolic filling and coronary perfusion time and can worsen, rather than protect, myocardial oxygen balance.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “15 mm Hg” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recall the determinants of myocardial oxygen supply and demand relevant to anesthetic management.

**C — correct:** 50 mm Hg

The stated formula is CPP = aortic diastolic pressure − LVEDP = 65 mmHg − 15 mmHg = 50 mmHg. This is a direct application of the formula as given in the source: 'The coronary perfusion pressure for the left ventricle is the aortic blood pressure during diastole minus the LV end-­diastolic pressure (LVEDP).'

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recall the determinants of myocardial oxygen supply and demand relevant to anesthetic management.

**D:** 65 mm Hg

A low arterial pressure alone does not establish decreased venous return; in this patient, low diastolic pressure directly lowers left ventricular coronary perfusion pressure.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “65 mm Hg” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recall the determinants of myocardial oxygen supply and demand relevant to anesthetic management.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 1936, passage 289609
- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 2370, passage 291742
- Barash 9e (2023).pdf — PDF p. 3287, passage 248886
- Barash 9e (2023).pdf — PDF p. 967, passage 244884

## S01-Q013

Domain 2 · Cardiovascular physiology and hemodynamics

A monitor provides MAP, right atrial pressure, and cardiac output. Which relationship calculates systemic vascular resistance?

A. Relate resistance directly to blood viscosity and vessel length.

B. Relate resistance inversely to vessel radius to the fourth power.

C. Relate tissue flow directly to pressure difference and inversely to resistance.

D. (Mean arterial pressure − right atrial pressure) divided by cardiac output.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: D**

**A:** Relate resistance directly to blood viscosity and vessel length.

Resistance is directly proportional to blood viscosity and vessel length.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Relate resistance directly to blood viscosity and vessel length.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply arterial pressure and vascular-resistance relationships to interpret a change in systemic hemodynamics.

**B:** Relate resistance inversely to vessel radius to the fourth power.

Resistance is inversely proportional to the fourth power of vessel radius.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Relate resistance inversely to vessel radius to the fourth power.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply arterial pressure and vascular-resistance relationships to interpret a change in systemic hemodynamics.

**C:** Relate tissue flow directly to pressure difference and inversely to resistance.

Tissue blood flow is directly proportional to pressure difference and inversely proportional to resistance.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Relate tissue flow directly to pressure difference and inversely to resistance.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply arterial pressure and vascular-resistance relationships to interpret a change in systemic hemodynamics.

**D — correct:** (Mean arterial pressure − right atrial pressure) divided by cardiac output.

Systemic vascular resistance is calculated as mean arterial pressure minus right atrial pressure, divided by cardiac output.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Apply arterial pressure and vascular-resistance relationships to interpret a change in systemic hemodynamics.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf — PDF p. 1184, passage 313797

## S01-Q014

Domain 2 · Ischemic heart disease and perioperative risk

A coronary patient develops elevated LV filling pressure during anesthesia. Which direct consequence most compromises perfusion of the inner ventricular myocardium?

A. Elevated LV end-diastolic pressure impairs blood flow to the inner ventricular myocardium.

B. Higher blood pressure raises coronary flow but also increases afterload, wall tension, and oxygen demand.

C. Coronary arterial blood flow is greatest during diastole.

D. LV coronary perfusion depends on the gradient between aortic diastolic pressure and LVEDP.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Elevated LV end-diastolic pressure impairs blood flow to the inner ventricular myocardium.

Elevations in LV end-diastolic pressure impede subendocardial blood flow.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate coronary perfusion pressure, ventricular filling pressure, diastolic duration, and afterload to identify hemodynamic changes that worsen myocardial ischemia.

**B:** Higher blood pressure raises coronary flow but also increases afterload, wall tension, and oxygen demand.

A rise in blood pressure increases coronary blood flow but also increases afterload, wall tension, and oxygen demand.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Higher blood pressure raises coronary flow but also increases afterload, wall tension, and oxygen demand.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate coronary perfusion pressure, ventricular filling pressure, diastolic duration, and afterload to identify hemodynamic changes that worsen myocardial ischemia.

**C:** Coronary arterial blood flow is greatest during diastole.

Seventy to eighty percent of coronary arterial blood flow occurs during diastole.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Coronary arterial blood flow is greatest during diastole.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate coronary perfusion pressure, ventricular filling pressure, diastolic duration, and afterload to identify hemodynamic changes that worsen myocardial ischemia.

**D:** LV coronary perfusion depends on the gradient between aortic diastolic pressure and LVEDP.

Left-ventricular coronary perfusion pressure is aortic diastolic pressure minus LV end-diastolic pressure.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “LV coronary perfusion depends on the gradient between aortic diastolic pressure and LVEDP.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate coronary perfusion pressure, ventricular filling pressure, diastolic duration, and afterload to identify hemodynamic changes that worsen myocardial ischemia.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 1936, passage 289609

## S01-Q015

Domain 2 · Ischemic heart disease and perioperative risk

Which option states the passage-defined threshold-based ECG characterization of myocardial ischemia?

A. Traditional ischemia monitoring uses leads II and V5.

B. Myocardial ischemia is characterized by ST-segment elevation or depression of at least 1 mm with T-wave inversion.

C. Greater ST-segment depression parallels greater ischemia severity.

D. Lead V5 reflects ischemia in LAD-supplied left-ventricular myocardium.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: SECOND PASS RESOLVED**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Traditional ischemia monitoring uses leads II and V5.

Monitoring leads II and V5 is a traditional lead-selection practice, not the threshold-based ECG characterization requested.

**B — correct:** Myocardial ischemia is characterized by ST-segment elevation or depression of at least 1 mm with T-wave inversion.

ST-segment elevation or depression of at least 1 mm with T-wave inversion is the best answer because it supplies the passage-defined threshold-based characterization of myocardial ischemia.

**C:** Greater ST-segment depression parallels greater ischemia severity.

Greater ST-segment depression may parallel greater ischemia severity, but this is a severity relationship rather than the requested diagnostic characterization and threshold.

**D:** Lead V5 reflects ischemia in LAD-supplied left-ventricular myocardium.

Lead V5 reflects LAD-supplied left-ventricular myocardium, but this describes an anatomic monitoring territory rather than the requested ECG threshold.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf — PDF p. 117, passage 232760

## S01-Q016

Domain 2 · Ischemic heart disease and perioperative risk

A patient is two weeks after elective PCI with a bare-metal stent for chronic coronary disease and is scheduled for an elective low-bleeding-risk noncardiac operation. Which perioperative plan best follows current guidance?

A. For surgery during the first 6 weeks after stenting, recognize this as the highest cardiovascular-risk interval when planning timing.

B. For PCI at least 12 months earlier, continue aspirin perioperatively if bleeding risk permits.

C. Delay elective noncardiac surgery until at least 1 month after PCI and continue DAPT during that interval.

D. For time-sensitive surgery within 30 days of BMS placement, generally continue DAPT unless bleeding risk outweighs benefit.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: C**

**A:** For surgery during the first 6 weeks after stenting, recognize this as the highest cardiovascular-risk interval when planning timing.

The risk of adverse cardiovascular outcomes after coronary stenting appears highest during the first 6 weeks.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “For surgery during the first 6 weeks after stenting, recognize this as the highest cardiovascular-risk interval when planning timing.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply antiplatelet and stent-timing principles when planning noncardiac surgery after coronary intervention.

**B:** For PCI at least 12 months earlier, continue aspirin perioperatively if bleeding risk permits.

When PCI occurred at least 12 months earlier, aspirin should be continued perioperatively if bleeding risk allows.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “For PCI at least 12 months earlier, continue aspirin perioperatively if bleeding risk permits.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply antiplatelet and stent-timing principles when planning noncardiac surgery after coronary intervention.

**C — correct:** Delay elective noncardiac surgery until at least 1 month after PCI and continue DAPT during that interval.

After elective PCI with a bare-metal stent for chronic coronary disease, noncardiac surgery should generally be delayed at least 1 month while DAPT is continued.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Apply antiplatelet and stent-timing principles when planning noncardiac surgery after coronary intervention.

**D:** For time-sensitive surgery within 30 days of BMS placement, generally continue DAPT unless bleeding risk outweighs benefit.

If time-sensitive surgery is required within 30 days of bare-metal stent placement, DAPT generally continues unless bleeding risk outweighs stent-thrombosis prevention.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “For time-sensitive surgery within 30 days of BMS placement, generally continue DAPT unless bleeding risk outweighs benefit.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply antiplatelet and stent-timing principles when planning noncardiac surgery after coronary intervention.

**Evidence sources:**

- 95__Perioperative_Management_of_Antiplatelet_Therapy.html — PDF p. 1, passage 316759

## S01-Q017

Domain 2 · Ischemic heart disease and perioperative risk

In a patient without a coronary stent undergoing high-bleeding-risk noncardiac surgery, aspirin interruption is chosen. What schedule is suggested?

A. Continue aspirin for minimal or low/moderate bleeding-risk surgery.

B. Do not start aspirin before surgery in a previous non-user to reduce perioperative events.

C. Continue aspirin for carotid endarterectomy or recent acute coronary syndrome or stroke.

D. Interrupt aspirin for 7 days and resume it when surgical bleeding risk has passed, typically within 24 hours.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: D**

**A:** Continue aspirin for minimal or low/moderate bleeding-risk surgery.

For minimal or low/moderate bleeding-risk surgery, perioperative aspirin continuation is listed as a clinical situation for continuation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Continue aspirin for minimal or low/moderate bleeding-risk surgery.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate perioperative risk factors to identify the patient most likely to benefit from targeted cardiac evaluation.

**B:** Do not start aspirin before surgery in a previous non-user to reduce perioperative events.

Starting aspirin before surgery in previous non-users to reduce perioperative cardiovascular events is not recommended.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Do not start aspirin before surgery in a previous non-user to reduce perioperative events.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate perioperative risk factors to identify the patient most likely to benefit from targeted cardiac evaluation.

**C:** Continue aspirin for carotid endarterectomy or recent acute coronary syndrome or stroke.

Aspirin continuation may be appropriate for carotid endarterectomy or recent acute coronary syndrome or stroke.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Continue aspirin for carotid endarterectomy or recent acute coronary syndrome or stroke.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate perioperative risk factors to identify the patient most likely to benefit from targeted cardiac evaluation.

**D — correct:** Interrupt aspirin for 7 days and resume it when surgical bleeding risk has passed, typically within 24 hours.

If aspirin interruption is chosen for a high-bleeding-risk noncardiac operation, interruption for 7 days is suggested and resumption is typically within 24 hours after bleeding risk passes.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate perioperative risk factors to identify the patient most likely to benefit from targeted cardiac evaluation.

**Evidence sources:**

- 95__Perioperative_Management_of_Antiplatelet_Therapy.html — PDF p. 1, passage 316757

## S01-Q018

Domain 2 · Heart failure and cardiomyopathy

A patient with reduced LV ejection is assessed before anaesthesia. Which physiologic characterization identifies HFrEF?

A. Left-ventricular systolic dysfunction.

B. Characterize HFpEF by LV diastolic dysfunction and ventricular and arterial stiffening.

C. Define heart failure as inadequate ejection or elevated-pressure filling.

D. Define diastolic function as effective filling at normal pressure.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Left-ventricular systolic dysfunction.

Heart failure with reduced ejection fraction is characterized by left-ventricular systolic dysfunction.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Apply heart-failure physiology to choose an anesthetic hemodynamic goal in reduced ejection fraction.

**B:** Characterize HFpEF by LV diastolic dysfunction and ventricular and arterial stiffening.

Left-ventricular diastolic dysfunction and ventricular and arterial stiffening are common in HFpEF.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Characterize HFpEF by LV diastolic dysfunction and ventricular and arterial stiffening.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply heart-failure physiology to choose an anesthetic hemodynamic goal in reduced ejection fraction.

**C:** Define heart failure as inadequate ejection or elevated-pressure filling.

Heart failure occurs when the LV cannot eject sufficiently to meet metabolic demands or cannot fill without elevated pressure.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Define heart failure as inadequate ejection or elevated-pressure filling.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply heart-failure physiology to choose an anesthetic hemodynamic goal in reduced ejection fraction.

**D:** Define diastolic function as effective filling at normal pressure.

Diastolic function is the ability of a cardiac chamber to collect blood effectively at normal filling pressure.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Define diastolic function as effective filling at normal pressure.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply heart-failure physiology to choose an anesthetic hemodynamic goal in reduced ejection fraction.

**Evidence sources:**

- Barash 9e (2023).pdf — PDF p. 841, passage 244672

Heart failure with reduced ejection fraction is defined in this passage as an ejection fraction of 40% or less.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate clinical findings to distinguish systolic from diastolic heart-failure physiology and its anesthetic implications.

**C:** An EF of 50% or more defines HFpEF.

HFpEF is diagnosed in patients with an ejection fraction of 50% or more.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “An EF of 50% or more defines HFpEF.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate clinical findings to distinguish systolic from diastolic heart-failure physiology and its anesthetic implications.

**D:** Diastolic dysfunction occurs in both HFrEF and HFpEF.

Diastolic dysfunction is present in both HFrEF and HFpEF.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Diastolic dysfunction occurs in both HFrEF and HFpEF.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate clinical findings to distinguish systolic from diastolic heart-failure physiology and its anesthetic implications.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf — PDF p. 214, passage 233176

Domain 2 · Heart failure and cardiomyopathy

During induction planning for hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, which finding represents the central obstructive mechanism that should guide management?

A. The murmur becomes louder with Valsalva or standing.

B. An LV outflow gradient of at least 30 mm Hg is clinically significant.

C. Dynamic left-ventricular outflow tract obstruction.

The hypertrophic cardiomyopathy murmur becomes louder with Valsalva or standing.

**B:** An LV outflow gradient of at least 30 mm Hg is clinically significant.

A left-ventricular outflow obstruction gradient of 30 mm Hg or more is clinically significant.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “An LV outflow gradient of at least 30 mm Hg is clinically significant.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recall the characteristic hemodynamic consequence of hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy relevant to induction.

**C — correct:** Dynamic left-ventricular outflow tract obstruction.

Dynamic left-ventricular outflow tract obstruction is a hallmark of hypertrophic cardiomyopathy.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recall the characteristic hemodynamic consequence of hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy relevant to induction.

**D:** A premature ventricular contraction increases the obstruction gradient.

The obstruction gradient typically increases after a premature ventricular contraction.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “A premature ventricular contraction increases the obstruction gradient.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recall the characteristic hemodynamic consequence of hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy relevant to induction.

**Evidence sources:**

- Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf — PDF p. 1084, passage 275071

## S01-Q032

Domain 3 · Quality human factors and systems safety

During a structured cardiac-surgery ICU handoff, which sequencing principle is violated when information transfer begins before equipment and monitoring checks are complete?

A. Overlap phase-1 and phase-2 tasks to avoid increasing handoff duration

B. Begin information transfer while equipment checks continue because protocols reduce omissions

C. Begin phase-2 patient information before completing phase-1 checks

D. Complete phase-1 equipment and monitoring checks before phase-2 information transfer.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: D**

**A:** Overlap phase-1 and phase-2 tasks to avoid increasing handoff duration

Using formal, sequential handoff procedures does not increase the duration of the handoff process.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Overlap phase-1 and phase-2 tasks to avoid increasing handoff duration” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply a structured handoff protocol to reduce information loss during intraoperative or ICU transfer of care

**B:** Begin information transfer while equipment checks continue because protocols reduce omissions

Implementing a handoff protocol reduces information omission and reduces errors.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Begin information transfer while equipment checks continue because protocols reduce omissions” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply a structured handoff protocol to reduce information loss during intraoperative or ICU transfer of care

**C:** Begin phase-2 patient information before completing phase-1 checks

During phase 2 of the handoff, the anesthesiologist presents patient-specific information including age, weight, medical and surgical history, allergy status, baseline vital signs, pertinent laboratory results, diagnosis, current condition, and vital signs.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Begin phase-2 patient information before completing phase-1 checks” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply a structured handoff protocol to reduce information loss during intraoperative or ICU transfer of care

**D — correct:** Complete phase-1 equipment and monitoring checks before phase-2 information transfer.

The handoff process is intended to be strictly sequential, with monitoring transferred before ventilator transfer, and all phase 1 items completed before information transfer.

## S01-Q033

Domain 3 · Quality human factors and systems safety

In a 2021 Canadian review of airway-related anesthesia malpractice claims, which category was reported in more than half of cases?

A. Faulty clinical decision-making.

B. Psychomotor impairment resembling alcohol or drug intoxication after sleep deprivation.

C. Individual performance effects of distraction, sleep deprivation, noise, illness, and aging.

D. Personal factors such as expertise, distraction, fatigue, and production pressure.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Faulty clinical decision-making.

In a 2021 Canadian review of anesthesiology airway-related medicolegal cases, faulty clinical decision-making was identified as a contributing factor in 54% of cases.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate human-factors principles (fatigue, workload, distraction) to explain how systems design contributes to anesthesia errors

**B:** Psychomotor impairment resembling alcohol or drug intoxication after sleep deprivation.

The psychomotor changes observed in sleep-deprived workers in the workplace are similar in many ways to those seen with alcohol or drug intoxication.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Psychomotor impairment resembling alcohol or drug intoxication after sleep deprivation.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate human-factors principles (fatigue, workload, distraction) to explain how systems design contributes to anesthesia errors

**C:** Individual performance effects of distraction, sleep deprivation, noise, illness, and aging.

Individual anesthesia provider performance can be adversely influenced by distractions, sleep deprivation, fatigue, environmental noise, illness, mental acuity, aging, and social dynamics within and between teams.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Individual performance effects of distraction, sleep deprivation, noise, illness, and aging.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate human-factors principles (fatigue, workload, distraction) to explain how systems design contributes to anesthesia errors

**D:** Personal factors such as expertise, distraction, fatigue, and production pressure.

Personal factors that impact anesthesia care and decision-making include level of expertise or familiarity with the procedure, other distractions, fatigue, and production pressure, all of which can predispose to errors.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Personal factors such as expertise, distraction, fatigue, and production pressure.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate human-factors principles (fatigue, workload, distraction) to explain how systems design contributes to anesthesia errors

## S01-Q034

Domain 3 · Quality human factors and systems safety

A hospital risk-management team is educating anesthesiologists about disclosing adverse events to patients under a just-culture framework. They want to highlight the specific evidence-based rationale for why timely, honest disclosure is advantageous from a medicolegal standpoint, as distinct from other benefits of a just-culture reporting system. Which statement reflects this medicolegal rationale?

A. Disclosure should be limited to facts known at the time, since discovery is ongoing

B. Timely disclosure of an adverse outcome may reduce legal risk

C. It encourages voluntary event reporting without threat of punishment

D. It reduces the likelihood patients change doctors, and improves satisfaction and trust

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Disclosure should be limited to facts known at the time, since discovery is ongoing

Experts advise limiting disclosure after an adverse event to factual information known at the time of the discussion, noting that discovery is ongoing and information may evolve.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Disclosure should be limited to facts known at the time, since discovery is ongoing” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply principles of transparent disclosure and just-culture reporting after a perioperative adverse event

**B — correct:** Timely disclosure of an adverse outcome may reduce legal risk

Emerging evidence suggests that timely disclosure of an adverse outcome may reduce legal risk.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Apply principles of transparent disclosure and just-culture reporting after a perioperative adverse event

**C:** It encourages voluntary event reporting without threat of punishment

Departments should encourage voluntary reporting of adverse events and near misses without the threat of punishment.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “It encourages voluntary event reporting without threat of punishment” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply principles of transparent disclosure and just-culture reporting after a perioperative adverse event

**D:** It reduces the likelihood patients change doctors, and improves satisfaction and trust

Studies suggest that disclosure of a medical error reduces the likelihood that patients will change doctors, improves patients' satisfaction, increases trust in the physician, and leads to a more positive emotional response.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “It reduces the likelihood patients change doctors, and improves satisfaction and trust” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply principles of transparent disclosure and just-culture reporting after a perioperative adverse event

**Evidence sources:**

- Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf — PDF p. 857, passage 274053

## S01-Q035

Domain 3 · Anesthetic complications and crises

During general anesthesia, a patient develops mild-to-moderate hypotension, tachycardia, and moderate bronchospasm shortly after a new intravenous antibiotic is administered, without severe hemodynamic collapse, severe angioedema, or loss of consciousness. According to standard severity-graded management, which treatment is indicated?

A. Epinephrine IM, escalating to an infusion if no response, with a 1 L crystalloid bolus and vasopressin for persistent hypotension beyond 10 minutes

B. Advanced cardiac life support methods

C. Epinephrine 0.01 mg/kg IM (maximum 0.5 mg adults, 0.3 mg children) into the thigh, plus a 0.5 L crystalloid bolus repeated if no response

D. No epinephrine; treat with antihistamines only

**Difficulty: Advanced**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: C**

**A:** Epinephrine IM, escalating to an infusion if no response, with a 1 L crystalloid bolus and vasopressin for persistent hypotension beyond 10 minutes

For grade 4 (severe) anaphylaxis, epinephrine 0.01 mg/kg IM is given, and if there is no response, an epinephrine infusion is started, along with a 1 L crystalloid bolus repeated if no response and vasopressin for persistent hypotension lasting more than 10 minutes.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Epinephrine IM, escalating to an infusion if no response, with a 1 L crystalloid bolus and vasopressin for persistent hypotension beyond 10 minutes” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate clinical signs and monitor data to rapidly diagnose and manage a perioperative anaphylaxis crisis

**B:** Advanced cardiac life support methods

For grade 5 anaphylaxis presenting as cardiopulmonary arrest, advanced cardiac life support methods are used.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Advanced cardiac life support methods” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate clinical signs and monitor data to rapidly diagnose and manage a perioperative anaphylaxis crisis

**C — correct:** Epinephrine 0.01 mg/kg IM (maximum 0.5 mg adults, 0.3 mg children) into the thigh, plus a 0.5 L crystalloid bolus repeated if no response

For grade 3 (mild-to-moderate) intraoperative anaphylaxis, epinephrine 0.01 mg/kg IM (maximum 0.5 mg in adults and 0.3 mg in children) is given into the thigh, along with a 0.5 L crystalloid bolus repeated if there is no response.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate clinical signs and monitor data to rapidly diagnose and manage a perioperative anaphylaxis crisis

**D:** No epinephrine; treat with antihistamines only

For grade 1 or 2 hypersensitivity reactions such as generalized urticaria or angioedema without hemodynamic compromise, epinephrine is not needed and antihistamines are used instead.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “No epinephrine; treat with antihistamines only” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate clinical signs and monitor data to rapidly diagnose and manage a perioperative anaphylaxis crisis

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 1095, passage 285644

## S01-Q040

Domain 3 · Ethics consent and professionalism

A physician disagrees with a capacitated patient’s decision to refuse an elective treatment. Which principle prevents the physician from equating an apparently undesirable decision with incapacity?

A. The majority of patients referred for capacity evaluations are found to have decision-making capacity.

B. Capacity assessment tools have been shown to be more likely to classify a female or non-White patient as lacking capacity.

C. Treatment refusal may simply reflect a patient's prioritization of other values above medical outcomes rather than a problem with capacity.

D. Functional capacity for decision-making must be judged separately from the perceived quality of the decision itself.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: SECOND PASS RESOLVED**

**Answer: D**

**A:** The majority of patients referred for capacity evaluations are found to have decision-making capacity.

The proportion of referred patients ultimately found to have capacity does not determine how this patient’s decision should be assessed.

**B:** Capacity assessment tools have been shown to be more likely to classify a female or non-White patient as lacking capacity.

Potential demographic bias in capacity tools is important, but it does not state the principle that separates an undesirable decision from incapacity.

**C:** Treatment refusal may simply reflect a patient's prioritization of other values above medical outcomes rather than a problem with capacity.

Treatment refusal may reflect values that outweigh medical outcomes, but this explains one possible reason for refusal rather than the controlling assessment principle asked for.

**D — correct:** Functional capacity for decision-making must be judged separately from the perceived quality of the decision itself.

Functional decision-making capacity must be judged separately from the perceived quality of the decision; this is the best answer because disagreement with the outcome cannot itself establish incapacity.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 153, passage 281155

## S01-Q051

Domain 4 · Shock and sepsis

A patient with early sepsis is warm peripherally and hypotensive. Cardiac output is increased. Which hemodynamic profile best describes this form of shock?

A. Low cardiac output with increased peripheral resistance due to volume loss

B. Reduced systemic vascular resistance with increased cardiac output

C. Low cardiac output with increased systemic vascular resistance due to pump failure

D. Obstruction to forward flow from tamponade, tension pneumothorax, or pulmonary embolism

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

Distributive shock is characterized by reduced systemic vascular resistance and often increased cardiac output, as in sepsis.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Recognize the characteristic early distributive-shock physiology of sepsis, including reduced systemic vascular resistance and relatively preserved or increased cardiac output.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Obstruction to forward flow from tamponade, tension pneumothorax, or pulmonary embolism” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Recognize the characteristic early distributive-shock physiology of sepsis, including reduced systemic vascular resistance and relatively preserved or increased cardiac output.

## S01-Q096

Domain 7 · Maternal physiology and pathology

A term pregnant patient becomes hypotensive when placed supine. Which statement provides the complete causal interpretation linking the position, gravid uterus, and blood-pressure decrease?

A. Neuraxial or general anaesthesia can impair the compensatory sympathetic response to supine hypotension.

B. At term, the inferior vena cava may be nearly completely occluded in the supine position.

C. Collateral venous return can occur through epidural, azygos, and vertebral veins.

D. In the supine position, blood pressure commonly decreases as the result of aortocaval compression by the gravid uterus.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: SECOND PASS RESOLVED**

**Answer: D**

**A:** Neuraxial or general anaesthesia can impair the compensatory sympathetic response to supine hypotension.

Anesthesia may impair sympathetic compensation and worsen supine hypotension, but it is a modifier and does not provide the complete position-to-uterus causal relationship.

**B:** At term, the inferior vena cava may be nearly completely occluded in the supine position.

Near-complete inferior-vena-cava occlusion is an anatomic component of the mechanism, but this option does not state the complete causal interpretation linking it to the blood-pressure decrease.

**C:** Collateral venous return can occur through epidural, azygos, and vertebral veins.

Collateral venous return can partially compensate through epidural, azygos, and vertebral veins; it does not explain why blood pressure falls.

**D — correct:** In the supine position, blood pressure commonly decreases as the result of aortocaval compression by the gravid uterus.

Aortocaval compression by the gravid uterus in the supine position is the best answer because it directly links position and uterine compression to the maternal blood-pressure decrease.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf — PDF p. 2691, passage 316105

## S01-Q129

Which combined change includes both reduced oxygen reserve and increased oxygen consumption, explaining rapid pediatric desaturation during emergence from general anesthesia?

B. Hypoxaemia may result from hypoventilation, upper-airway obstruction, bronchospasm, aspiration, edema, pneumothorax, atelectasis, shunting, or embolism.

## S01-Q006

Domain 1 · Airway devices and intubation

After a video laryngoscope provides a good glottic view, the endotracheal tube still will not pass. Which device limitation best explains this problem?

A. An improved Cormack-Lehane view guarantees uncomplicated tube passage

B. A rescue-role device should be abandoned once the glottis is visible

C. The tube may remain difficult to pass despite a good glottic view.

D. Adequate mouth opening eliminates tube-delivery difficulty

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: C**

**A:** An improved Cormack-Lehane view guarantees uncomplicated tube passage

Video laryngoscopy can improve Cormack and Lehane grade 1–2 views.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “An improved Cormack-Lehane view guarantees uncomplicated tube passage” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate device limitations and tube-delivery technique when using a video laryngoscope.

**B:** A rescue-role device should be abandoned once the glottis is visible

Video laryngoscopy can serve as a rescue technique in a patient with a difficult airway.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “A rescue-role device should be abandoned once the glottis is visible” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate device limitations and tube-delivery technique when using a video laryngoscope.

**C — correct:** The tube may remain difficult to pass despite a good glottic view.

A video laryngoscope may show the glottis well while passage of the endotracheal tube remains difficult.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate device limitations and tube-delivery technique when using a video laryngoscope.

**D:** Adequate mouth opening eliminates tube-delivery difficulty

Adequate mouth opening is required for video-laryngoscope use.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Adequate mouth opening eliminates tube-delivery difficulty” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate device limitations and tube-delivery technique when using a video laryngoscope.

**Evidence sources:**

- Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf — PDF p. 774, passage 273668

## S01-Q007

Domain 1 · Difficult-airway rescue

After failed tracheal intubation, oxygen saturation is falling and face-mask ventilation is inadequate. Which immediate rescue action best restores oxygenation?

A. Pause for a repeat Mallampati and mandibular-protrusion assessment

B. Place an airway-exchange catheter in the midtrachea before restoring oxygenation

C. Continue cautious intubation attempts without introducing a rescue oxygenation device

D. Insert a supraglottic airway such as a laryngeal mask airway

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: D**

**A:** Pause for a repeat Mallampati and mandibular-protrusion assessment

Airway assessment includes Mallampati class, thyromental distance, atlanto-occipital extension, and mandibular protrusion.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Pause for a repeat Mallampati and mandibular-protrusion assessment” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply a difficult-airway rescue algorithm to restore oxygenation after failed tracheal intubation.

**B:** Place an airway-exchange catheter in the midtrachea before restoring oxygenation

An airway exchange catheter is ideally positioned with its distal tip in the midtrachea.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Place an airway-exchange catheter in the midtrachea before restoring oxygenation” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply a difficult-airway rescue algorithm to restore oxygenation after failed tracheal intubation.

**C:** Continue cautious intubation attempts without introducing a rescue oxygenation device

Both intubation and extubation require caution when difficult-airway risk factors are present.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Continue cautious intubation attempts without introducing a rescue oxygenation device” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply a difficult-airway rescue algorithm to restore oxygenation after failed tracheal intubation.

**D — correct:** Insert a supraglottic airway such as a laryngeal mask airway

A supraglottic airway such as a laryngeal mask is the rescue device of choice in an unanticipated difficult airway when oxygenation is failing.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Apply a difficult-airway rescue algorithm to restore oxygenation after failed tracheal intubation.

**Evidence sources:**

- Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf — PDF p. 784, passage 273713

## S01-Q008

Domain 1 · Difficult-airway rescue

According to the Difficult Airway Society (DAS) 2025 guidelines, after failed intubation an elective case can be deferred and a second-generation supraglottic airway provides effective ventilation. What is the default next step?

A. Discontinue anaesthesia and wake the patient up.

B. Continue the procedure using the supraglottic airway device.

C. Intubate through the supraglottic airway device with a bronchoscope.

D. Intubate blindly through the supraglottic airway device.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Discontinue anaesthesia and wake the patient up.

The guideline states: 'After effective oxygenation through a SAD, the default decision should be to discontinue anaesthesia and wake the patient up, unless proceeding is essential.' The stem specifies proceeding is not essential, matching the guideline's stated default condition.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Choose the next rescue action when a patient cannot be intubated but can be oxygenated.

**B:** Continue the procedure using the supraglottic airway device.

The guideline permits this option only 'If proceeding is essential and ventilation through the supraglottic airway device is satisfactory, consider continuing with the supraglottic airway device.' The stem states proceeding is not essential, so this branch's stated condition is not met.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Continue the procedure using the supraglottic airway device.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Choose the next rescue action when a patient cannot be intubated but can be oxygenated.

**C:** Intubate through the supraglottic airway device with a bronchoscope.

The guideline permits this option only 'If tracheal intubation is essential, consider one attempt at intubation through the supraglottic airway device using a flexible bronchoscope.' The stem does not state that tracheal intubation is essential, so this branch's stated condition is not met.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Intubate through the supraglottic airway device with a bronchoscope.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Choose the next rescue action when a patient cannot be intubated but can be oxygenated.

**D:** Intubate blindly through the supraglottic airway device.

The guideline states plainly: 'Blind tracheal intubation through a supraglottic airway device is not recommended.' This option is explicitly excluded regardless of the surgical urgency described in the stem.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Intubate blindly through the supraglottic airway device.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Choose the next rescue action when a patient cannot be intubated but can be oxygenated.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf — PDF p. 399, passage 233940
- DAS_2025_Unanticipated_Difficult_Intubation_Adults.pdf — PDF p. 10, passage 317285

## S01-Q009

Domain 1 · Extubation and complications

A patient with a potentially difficult airway has airway oedema after prolonged surgery and is ready for emergence. Which plan is essential before extubation?

A. Extubate when awake to recover pharyngeal tone and airway reflexes.

B. Formulate the extubation plan in advance and include a plan for reintubation.

C. Use deep extubation to avoid coughing and haemodynamic effects.

D. Avoid extubation during a light stage-II plane of anaesthesia.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Extubate when awake to recover pharyngeal tone and airway reflexes.

An awake patient more easily maintains a patent airway because pharyngeal tone and airway reflexes recover.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Extubate when awake to recover pharyngeal tone and airway reflexes.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate risk assessment and planning principles for extubation of a patient with a potentially difficult airway.

**B — correct:** Formulate the extubation plan in advance and include a plan for reintubation.

An extubation plan should be formulated in advance and include a reintubation plan if the airway cannot be maintained.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate risk assessment and planning principles for extubation of a patient with a potentially difficult airway.

**C:** Use deep extubation to avoid coughing and haemodynamic effects.

Deep extubation avoids coughing and adverse hemodynamic effects but risks upper-airway obstruction and hypoventilation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Use deep extubation to avoid coughing and haemodynamic effects.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate risk assessment and planning principles for extubation of a patient with a potentially difficult airway.

**D:** Avoid extubation during a light stage-II plane of anaesthesia.

Extubation during a light stage-II plane of anesthesia increases laryngospasm and other airway complications and should be avoided.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Avoid extubation during a light stage-II plane of anaesthesia.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate risk assessment and planning principles for extubation of a patient with a potentially difficult airway.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 1512, passage 287594

## S01-Q010

Domain 1 · Extubation and complications

An adult in the PACU received fentanyl intraoperatively. Immediately after tracheal extubation, the patient develops snoring, paradoxical chest-abdominal movement, and falling oxygen saturation. What is the most appropriate initial action?

A. Administer titrated naloxone for possible opioid sedation.

B. Reintubate the trachea to secure the airway.

C. Apply jaw thrust together with face-mask CPAP.

D. Insert an oral airway to relieve obstruction.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: C**

**A:** Administer titrated naloxone for possible opioid sedation.

Miller places identification and treatment of the cause after the upper airway has been opened and adequate ventilation ensured; airway opening therefore remains the first priority.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Administer titrated naloxone for possible opioid sedation.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply immediate management priorities to post-extubation upper-airway obstruction.

**B:** Reintubate the trachea to secure the airway.

Miller directs clinicians to attempt noninvasive airway-opening measures before tracheal reintubation. No noninvasive maneuver has yet been attempted in this patient.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Reintubate the trachea to secure the airway.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply immediate management priorities to post-extubation upper-airway obstruction.

**C — correct:** Apply jaw thrust together with face-mask CPAP.

Miller's Anesthesia, 10th edition states that an obstructed upper airway requires immediate attention, that noninvasive measures should precede reintubation, and that jaw thrust with CPAP is often sufficient to open an upper airway obstructed by decreased pharyngeal muscle tone.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Apply immediate management priorities to post-extubation upper-airway obstruction.

**D:** Insert an oral airway to relieve obstruction.

Miller places oral, nasal, or laryngeal mask airway insertion after CPAP is found ineffective. No trial of jaw thrust with CPAP has yet occurred, so this supported later step is premature.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Insert an oral airway to relieve obstruction.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply immediate management priorities to post-extubation upper-airway obstruction.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf — PDF p. 27, passage 232366
- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 2968, passage 294726
- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 2968, passage 294727
- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 2969, passage 294728

## S01-Q021

Domain 2 · Valvular disease

A patient with severe aortic stenosis is undergoing general anesthesia. Which of the following best describes the appropriate hemodynamic management strategy for this specific lesion?

A. Control heart rate while avoiding hypovolemia and hypervolemia to assure adequate perfusion pressure

B. Increase afterload, decrease contractility, and treat hypotension aggressively with α-adrenergic agonists

C. Maintain heart rate at the low end of normal, avoiding and treating tachycardia

D. Maintain sinus rhythm, a normal heart rate, and adequate preload, afterload, and contractility

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: D**

**A:** Control heart rate while avoiding hypovolemia and hypervolemia to assure adequate perfusion pressure

In coronary artery disease, the hemodynamic goals are to control heart rate, avoid hypo- and hypervolemia, and assure adequate perfusion pressure.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Control heart rate while avoiding hypovolemia and hypervolemia to assure adequate perfusion pressure” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply hemodynamic management goals for severe aortic stenosis during general anesthesia

**B:** Increase afterload, decrease contractility, and treat hypotension aggressively with α-adrenergic agonists

In hypertrophic cardiomyopathy, hemodynamic management should increase afterload and decrease contractility, treating hypotension aggressively with α-adrenergic agonists.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Increase afterload, decrease contractility, and treat hypotension aggressively with α-adrenergic agonists” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply hemodynamic management goals for severe aortic stenosis during general anesthesia

**C:** Maintain heart rate at the low end of normal, avoiding and treating tachycardia

In mitral stenosis, heart rate should be maintained at the low end of normal, avoiding and treating tachycardia.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Maintain heart rate at the low end of normal, avoiding and treating tachycardia” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Apply hemodynamic management goals for severe aortic stenosis during general anesthesia

**D — correct:** Maintain sinus rhythm, a normal heart rate, and adequate preload, afterload, and contractility

In patients with aortic stenosis, anesthetic management should include maintenance of sinus rhythm, a normal heart rate, and maintenance of adequate preload, afterload, and contractility.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Apply hemodynamic management goals for severe aortic stenosis during general anesthesia

## S01-Q022

Domain 2 · Valvular disease

In severe mitral stenosis, which combined perioperative plan best preserves forward flow and avoids physiologic deterioration?

A. Avoid tachyarrhythmias, hypervolemia, and further increases in right-ventricular afterload.

B. Postpone surgery until a new atrial-fibrillation rate is controlled.

C. Avoid myocardial depressants and vasodilators while maintaining preload and afterload.

D. Maintain SVR and preload while treating hypoxemia, hypercarbia, and acidosis.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Avoid tachyarrhythmias, hypervolemia, and further increases in right-ventricular afterload.

In mitral stenosis, tachyarrhythmias, hypervolemia, and further increases in right ventricular afterload should all be avoided.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate the combined anesthetic implications of severe mitral stenosis with pulmonary hypertension and atrial fibrillation to justify avoiding tachycardia, abrupt preload shifts, and increases in pulmonary vascular resistance.

**B:** Postpone surgery until a new atrial-fibrillation rate is controlled.

If new-onset atrial fibrillation occurs prior to induction of anesthesia, surgery should be postponed, if possible, until the ventricular rate is controlled or sinus rhythm is achieved.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Postpone surgery until a new atrial-fibrillation rate is controlled.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate the combined anesthetic implications of severe mitral stenosis with pulmonary hypertension and atrial fibrillation to justify avoiding tachycardia, abrupt preload shifts, and increases in pulmonary vascular resistance.

**C:** Avoid myocardial depressants and vasodilators while maintaining preload and afterload.

In high-risk aortic stenosis with LV dysfunction, management should avoid myocardial depressants and vasodilators, meticulously maintain preload and afterload, and avoid an abrupt decrease in SVR with sympathectomy.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Avoid myocardial depressants and vasodilators while maintaining preload and afterload.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate the combined anesthetic implications of severe mitral stenosis with pulmonary hypertension and atrial fibrillation to justify avoiding tachycardia, abrupt preload shifts, and increases in pulmonary vascular resistance.

**D:** Maintain SVR and preload while treating hypoxemia, hypercarbia, and acidosis.

In severe pulmonary hypertension, management should maintain SVR and preload/venous return, prevent or treat pain, hypoxemia, hypercarbia, and acidosis, and avoid myocardial depression.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Maintain SVR and preload while treating hypoxemia, hypercarbia, and acidosis.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate the combined anesthetic implications of severe mitral stenosis with pulmonary hypertension and atrial fibrillation to justify avoiding tachycardia, abrupt preload shifts, and increases in pulmonary vascular resistance.

- Chestnut 6e (2019).pdf — PDF p. 1063, passage 274981
- Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf — PDF p. 181, passage 233034

## S01-Q023

Domain 2 · Valvular disease

According to Thrombosis Canada: Warfarin Perioperative Management, which risk-stratified bridging plan is correct when warfarin is interrupted for elective surgery?

A. Continue warfarin as the preferred agent for rheumatic mitral stenosis.

B. Bridge high-risk patients, do not bridge low-risk patients, and individualize intermediate-risk patients.

C. Substitute a DOAC for warfarin in valvular atrial fibrillation.

D. Add aspirin routinely to warfarin perioperatively.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Continue warfarin as the preferred agent for rheumatic mitral stenosis.

Warfarin is used for prevention of stroke or systemic embolism in patients with atrial fibrillation, especially those with rheumatic mitral stenosis, where DOACs should be avoided.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Continue warfarin as the preferred agent for rheumatic mitral stenosis.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Determine appropriate perioperative interruption and bridging of anticoagulation for a patient with a mechanical prosthetic heart valve undergoing noncardiac surgery, based on thromboembolic risk stratification.

**B — correct:** Bridge high-risk patients, do not bridge low-risk patients, and individualize intermediate-risk patients.

Bridging anticoagulation during warfarin interruption is suggested in patients at high risk for thromboembolism, not suggested in low-risk patients, and optional in intermediate-risk patients based on individual patient characteristics.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Determine appropriate perioperative interruption and bridging of anticoagulation for a patient with a mechanical prosthetic heart valve undergoing noncardiac surgery, based on thromboembolic risk stratification.

**C:** Substitute a DOAC for warfarin in valvular atrial fibrillation.

Treatment with a DOAC is not recommended in patients with valvular atrial fibrillation, and these drugs are not approved for this use.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Substitute a DOAC for warfarin in valvular atrial fibrillation.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Determine appropriate perioperative interruption and bridging of anticoagulation for a patient with a mechanical prosthetic heart valve undergoing noncardiac surgery, based on thromboembolic risk stratification.

**D:** Add aspirin routinely to warfarin perioperatively.

Experts suggest combining ASA with warfarin in patients with mechanical heart valves, especially if the patient is at low risk of bleeding or at high thrombotic risk.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Add aspirin routinely to warfarin perioperatively.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Determine appropriate perioperative interruption and bridging of anticoagulation for a patient with a mechanical prosthetic heart valve undergoing noncardiac surgery, based on thromboembolic risk stratification.

## S01-Q024

Domain 2 · Arrhythmias and CIEDs

During intraoperative use of monopolar electrocautery, a magnet is applied over a patient's permanent pacemaker (the patient has no ICD). Which of the following best describes the expected device response?

A. The magnet disables antitachycardia function but does not convert the device to asynchronous pacing

B. The magnet inhibits tachyarrhythmia detection and prevents shock delivery, without changing the pacing mode

C. In a majority of pacemakers, magnet application induces an asynchronous mode

D. Asynchronous pacing will not occur unless the device is separately reprogrammed

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: C**

**A:** The magnet disables antitachycardia function but does not convert the device to asynchronous pacing

Magnet application to ICDs may disable the antitachycardia function but not convert the device to an asynchronous pacemaker.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “The magnet disables antitachycardia function but does not convert the device to asynchronous pacing” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Distinguish the fundamental effect of magnet application on a permanent pacemaker from its effect on an implantable cardioverter-defibrillator.

**B:** The magnet inhibits tachyarrhythmia detection and prevents shock delivery, without changing the pacing mode

Magnet application to an ICD inhibits tachyarrhythmia detection, thus preventing therapy (i.e., delivery of shocks), but it does not change the mode of the underlying pacemaker.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “The magnet inhibits tachyarrhythmia detection and prevents shock delivery, without changing the pacing mode” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Distinguish the fundamental effect of magnet application on a permanent pacemaker from its effect on an implantable cardioverter-defibrillator.

**C — correct:** In a majority of pacemakers, magnet application induces an asynchronous mode

In a majority of pacemakers, magnet application induces an asynchronous mode.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Distinguish the fundamental effect of magnet application on a permanent pacemaker from its effect on an implantable cardioverter-defibrillator.

**D:** Asynchronous pacing will not occur unless the device is separately reprogrammed

Asynchronous pacing from an ICD always requires reprogramming.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Asynchronous pacing will not occur unless the device is separately reprogrammed” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Distinguish the fundamental effect of magnet application on a permanent pacemaker from its effect on an implantable cardioverter-defibrillator.
