## S01-Q025

Domain 2 · Arrhythmias and CIEDs

A patient develops new-onset atrial fibrillation intraoperatively. Which of the following best describes how the choice between rate control and rhythm control should be determined?

A. Surgery should be postponed until the ventricular rate is controlled or sinus rhythm is achieved before deciding on further management

B. Esmolol should be used because it is equally effective for rate control and for increasing conversion to sinus rhythm

C. Rate control is always the immediate treatment goal regardless of the patient's hemodynamic status

D. Hemodynamically stable patients may be managed medically with rate or rhythm control, while hemodynamically unstable patients should be electrically cardioverted

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: D**

**A:** Surgery should be postponed until the ventricular rate is controlled or sinus rhythm is achieved before deciding on further management

If new-onset atrial fibrillation occurs prior to induction of anesthesia, surgery should be postponed, if possible, until the ventricular rate is controlled or sinus rhythm is achieved.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Surgery should be postponed until the ventricular rate is controlled or sinus rhythm is achieved before deciding on further management” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Reason through the choice between rate control and rhythm control for new-onset perioperative atrial fibrillation, contingent on the patient's hemodynamic stability.

**B:** Esmolol should be used because it is equally effective for rate control and for increasing conversion to sinus rhythm

Esmolol has been shown to be equally effective in controlling the ventricular rate in patients with postoperative atrial fibrillation or flutter and in increasing the conversion rate to regular sinus rhythm from 8% to 34%.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Esmolol should be used because it is equally effective for rate control and for increasing conversion to sinus rhythm” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Reason through the choice between rate control and rhythm control for new-onset perioperative atrial fibrillation, contingent on the patient's hemodynamic stability.

**C:** Rate control is always the immediate treatment goal regardless of the patient's hemodynamic status

Control of the ventricular response rate is the immediate goal in the treatment of new-onset atrial fibrillation.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Rate control is always the immediate treatment goal regardless of the patient's hemodynamic status” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Reason through the choice between rate control and rhythm control for new-onset perioperative atrial fibrillation, contingent on the patient's hemodynamic stability.

**D — correct:** Hemodynamically stable patients may be managed medically with rate or rhythm control, while hemodynamically unstable patients should be electrically cardioverted

Patients with new-onset postoperative atrial fibrillation who are hemodynamically stable may be managed medically with rate or rhythm control, while hemodynamically unstable patients should be electrically cardioverted.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Reason through the choice between rate control and rhythm control for new-onset perioperative atrial fibrillation, contingent on the patient's hemodynamic stability.

## S01-Q026

Domain 2 · Cardiac surgery and CPB

Which ACT threshold is described as the target used by most adult cardiac surgical centers during cardiopulmonary bypass?

A. An ACT longer than 400 seconds.

B. An ACT of 480 seconds as a pediatric pre-bypass target.

C. An ACT longer than 350 seconds as an alternative adequacy threshold.

D. An ACT below 300 seconds as a pediatric antithrombin-deficiency signal.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: A**

Most cardiac surgical centers target an ACT value longer than 400 seconds during cardiopulmonary bypass in adults.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Select and titrate anticoagulation (heparin dosing, ACT target) before initiating cardiopulmonary bypass

**B:** An ACT of 480 seconds as a pediatric pre-bypass target.

In pediatric cardiac surgery, the target ACT before initiating cardiopulmonary bypass is usually 480 seconds.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “An ACT of 480 seconds as a pediatric pre-bypass target.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select and titrate anticoagulation (heparin dosing, ACT target) before initiating cardiopulmonary bypass

**C:** An ACT longer than 350 seconds as an alternative adequacy threshold.

During cardiopulmonary bypass, an ACT value longer than 350 seconds is sometimes considered adequate, though this is not the target used by most centers.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “An ACT longer than 350 seconds as an alternative adequacy threshold.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select and titrate anticoagulation (heparin dosing, ACT target) before initiating cardiopulmonary bypass

**D:** An ACT below 300 seconds as a pediatric antithrombin-deficiency signal.

In children undergoing cardiopulmonary bypass, an ACT that remains less than 300 seconds despite a standard heparin dose raises suspicion for antithrombin III deficiency.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “An ACT below 300 seconds as a pediatric antithrombin-deficiency signal.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Select and titrate anticoagulation (heparin dosing, ACT target) before initiating cardiopulmonary bypass

Intraoperative infarction is a recognized ischemic etiology of right or left ventricular dysfunction after cardiopulmonary bypass.

**D:** Technical difficulties such as kinked or clotted grafts.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: State the criteria and physiologic checklist used to separate a patient from cardiopulmonary bypass

**D:** Assess vascular resistance, since CPB flow is proportional to MAP divided by resistance

Before separation from cardiopulmonary bypass, the checklist specifies that vascular resistance should be assessed because CPB flow is proportional to mean arterial pressure divided by resistance.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Assess vascular resistance, since CPB flow is proportional to MAP divided by resistance” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: State the criteria and physiologic checklist used to separate a patient from cardiopulmonary bypass

## S01-Q030

Domain 2 · Vascular aortic and carotid anesthesia

During carotid endarterectomy, which monitoring modality can determine shunt need, recognize shunt malfunction, and help manage postoperative hyperperfusion?

A. Transcranial Doppler ultrasonography.

B. SEPs as a direct shunt-decision test.

C. Stump pressure as a direct shunt-decision test.

D. Standard scalp EEG as a direct shunt-decision test.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Transcranial Doppler ultrasonography.

Transcranial Doppler ultrasonography can be used to determine the need for shunt placement, to recognize shunt malfunction, and to manage postoperative hyperperfusion during carotid endarterectomy.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: Integrate neuromonitoring and hemodynamic trends to detect and manage cerebral ischemia during carotid endarterectomy

**B:** SEPs as a direct shunt-decision test.

Somatosensory evoked potential monitoring can detect specific changes produced by decreased regional cerebral blood flow, but it can be difficult to determine whether these changes are due to anesthesia, hypothermia, changes in blood pressure, or cerebral ischemia.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “SEPs as a direct shunt-decision test.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate neuromonitoring and hemodynamic trends to detect and manage cerebral ischemia during carotid endarterectomy

**C:** Stump pressure as a direct shunt-decision test.

Stump pressure (internal carotid artery back pressure) is a poor indicator of the adequacy of cerebral perfusion during carotid endarterectomy.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Stump pressure as a direct shunt-decision test.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate neuromonitoring and hemodynamic trends to detect and manage cerebral ischemia during carotid endarterectomy

**D:** Standard scalp EEG as a direct shunt-decision test.

EEG may not detect subcortical or small cortical infarcts during carotid endarterectomy.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Standard scalp EEG as a direct shunt-decision test.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: Integrate neuromonitoring and hemodynamic trends to detect and manage cerebral ischemia during carotid endarterectomy

## S01-Q031

Domain 2 · Vascular aortic and carotid anesthesia

Which preoperative strategy can restore intravascular volume and reset cerebral autoregulation before elective carotid endarterectomy?

A. Increase pressure during awake neurologic change after carotid occlusion.

B. Gradually reduce arterial blood pressure over several weeks before surgery.

C. Accept pressure-dependent perfusion after acute stroke.

D. Induce hypertension during carotid clamping.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Increase pressure during awake neurologic change after carotid occlusion.

If a patient becomes agitated, confused, or unresponsive following carotid occlusion during awake carotid surgery, the anesthesiologist should increase systemic blood pressure up to 20% greater than preoperative values.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Increase pressure during awake neurologic change after carotid occlusion.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: State perioperative blood pressure targets and rationale for patients with carotid artery stenosis

**B — correct:** Gradually reduce arterial blood pressure over several weeks before surgery.

Gradual reduction of arterial blood pressure over several weeks before carotid endarterectomy restores intravascular volume, resets cerebral autoregulation to a more normal range, and improves perioperative management.

Best-answer reasoning: This option directly satisfies the tested objective: State perioperative blood pressure targets and rationale for patients with carotid artery stenosis

**C:** Accept pressure-dependent perfusion after acute stroke.

Cerebral autoregulation is impaired and cerebral perfusion varies with changes in blood pressure in the days to months following an acute stroke.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Accept pressure-dependent perfusion after acute stroke.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: State perioperative blood pressure targets and rationale for patients with carotid artery stenosis

**D:** Induce hypertension during carotid clamping.

In patients with contralateral internal carotid artery occlusion or severe stenosis, induced hypertension to approximately 10% to 20% above baseline is advocated during the period of carotid clamping when neurophysiologic monitoring is not used.

Why it is not best here: This option addresses “Induce hypertension during carotid clamping.” rather than satisfying the complete tested objective: State perioperative blood pressure targets and rationale for patients with carotid artery stenosis

## S01-Q043

Domain 3 · Statistics and evidence

A reviewer critically appraising a randomized controlled trial notes that despite randomization, patients in one treatment arm were more likely to drop out of the study before the outcome could be assessed. Which type of bias does this describe?

A. Attrition bias refers to systematic differences in the withdrawal of patients from each of the two intervention groups.

B. Detection bias refers to systematic differences in the ascertainment and recording of outcomes.

C. Selection bias in a randomized controlled trial refers to systematic differences between the patients receiving each intervention.

D. Performance bias refers to systematic differences in care given to study patients other than the preplanned interventions being evaluated.

**Difficulty: Foundational**

**First-pass status: RATIONALE UPDATED**

## S01-Q046

CNS excitation, depression, and nonspecific neurologic symptoms describe only one of the two principal organ systems requested.

**C:** Intravenous local-anesthetic administration produces the highest systemic absorption, while subcutaneous administration produces the lowest.

Intravenous administration produces the greatest systemic absorption, but this describes exposure pharmacokinetics rather than the affected organ systems.

**D:** Local-anesthetic toxicity is additive when injected at multiple sites.

Additive toxicity from injections at multiple sites is a dosing-risk principle rather than an identification of the affected organ systems.

## S01-Q226

Domain 13 · Thoracic procedures

After intrathoracic surgery, a mechanically ventilated patient develops sudden hypotension, rising airway pressures, difficult manual ventilation, unilateral absence of breath sounds, and a distended hemithorax. Tension pneumothorax is suspected. What is the most appropriate next management?

A. Perform immediate needle decompression followed by definitive chest-tube insertion.

B. Obtain confirmatory chest radiography before pleural decompression.

C. Increase positive end-expiratory pressure and observe for hemodynamic recovery.

D. Perform pericardiocentesis as the initial decompressive procedure.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**First-pass status: SECOND PASS RESOLVED**

**Answer: A**

**A — correct:** Perform immediate needle decompression followed by definitive chest-tube insertion.

Immediate decompression is the best answer because tension pneumothorax is a clinical diagnosis requiring treatment without waiting for imaging; needle decompression temporizes the emergency and chest-tube insertion provides definitive treatment.

**B:** Obtain confirmatory chest radiography before pleural decompression.

Treatment should not be delayed for radiologic confirmation when the clinical findings indicate tension pneumothorax.

**C:** Increase positive end-expiratory pressure and observe for hemodynamic recovery.

Additional positive airway pressure does not evacuate trapped pleural gas and may worsen impaired venous return; immediate pleural decompression is required.

**D:** Perform pericardiocentesis as the initial decompressive procedure.

Pericardiocentesis treats cardiac tamponade, whereas the unilateral respiratory findings and rising airway pressures indicate tension pneumothorax requiring pleural decompression.

**Evidence sources:**

- Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf — PDF p. 78, passage 232581
- Stoelting Coexisting 8e (2021).pdf — PDF p. 250, passage 233321

## S01-Q258

Domain 14 · Organ procurement

During organ procurement management, a brain-dead donor develops abrupt high-volume dilute urine output, rising serum sodium, and progressive hypovolemia. Which brain-death-associated disorder best explains this pattern?

A. Catecholamine storm from the initial sympathetic discharge.

B. Central diabetes insipidus from loss of posterior-pituitary function.

C. Vasoplegia from loss of sympathetic tone.

D. Hypothermia from loss of hypothalamic thermoregulation.

**Difficulty: Intermediate**

**First-pass status: SECOND PASS RESOLVED**

**Answer: B**

**A:** Catecholamine storm from the initial sympathetic discharge.

The catecholamine storm produces vasoconstriction, hypertension, and tachycardia rather than the dilute polyuria and hypernatremia described.

**B — correct:** Central diabetes insipidus from loss of posterior-pituitary function.

Central diabetes insipidus is the best answer because posterior-pituitary failure after brain death causes severe free-water and electrolyte derangements, producing high-volume dilute urine, hypernatremia, and hypovolemia.

**C:** Vasoplegia from loss of sympathetic tone.

Loss of sympathetic tone can cause hypotension and peripheral vasodilation, but it does not explain abrupt dilute polyuria with rising sodium.

**D:** Hypothermia from loss of hypothalamic thermoregulation.

Hypothalamic failure can cause hypothermia through reduced metabolism, vasodilation, and heat loss, but it does not produce the specified urine and sodium pattern.

**Evidence sources:**

- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 2231, passage 291040
- Miller 10e (2024).pdf — PDF p. 2230, passage 291038
- Stoelting P&P 6e (2022).pdf — PDF p. 2390, passage 315649
